### ConAct1.doc

Source: `9. TEACHER GUIDE/Grade 8/Solutions/Chapter 3 Word Solution Files/Final product/ConAct1.doc`

Legacy Word text transcription. Original visual formatting is not retained.

DolphinsDolphins are  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_mammal" \o "Marine mammal" marine mammals closely related to  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whale" \o "Whale" whales and  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porpoise" \o "Porpoise" porpoises. There are almost forty species of dolphin in 17  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genus" \o "Genus" genera. They vary in size from 1.2 m (4 ft) and 40 kg (90 lb) ( HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui%27s_dolphin" \o "Maui's dolphin" Maui's dolphin), up to 9.5 m (30 ft) and 10 tonnes (9.8 long tons; 11 short tons) (the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killer_whale" \o "Killer whale" orca or killer whale). They are found worldwide, mostly in the shallower seas of the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelves" \o "Continental shelves" continental shelves, and are carnivores, eating mostly  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish" \o "Fish" fish and  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squid" \o "Squid" squid. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_(biology)" \o "Family (biology)" family  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_dolphin" \o "Oceanic dolphin" Delphinidae is the largest in the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetacea" \o "Cetacea" Cetacean order, and evolved relatively recently, about ten million years ago, during the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene" \o "Miocene" Miocene. Dolphins are among the most intelligent animals, and their often friendly appearance, an artifact of the "smile" of their mouth line, and seemingly playful attitude have made them very popular in  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_culture" \o "Human culture" human culture.AnatomyDolphins have a streamlined  HYPERLINK "http://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/fusiform" \o "wikt:fusiform" fusiform body, adapted for fast swimming. The tail fin, called the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/fluke" \o "wiktionary:fluke" fluke, is used for  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_locomotion" \o "Animal locomotion" propulsion, while the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pectoral_fin" \o "Pectoral fin" pectoral fins together with the entire tail section provide directional control. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorsal_fin" \o "Dorsal fin" dorsal fin, in those species that have one, provides stability while swimming.The head contains the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melon_(whale)" \o "Melon (whale)" melon, a round organ used for  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_echolocation" \o "Animal echolocation" echolocation. In many species, elongated jaws form a distinct beak; species such as the bottlenose have a curved mouth which looks like a fixed smile. Some species have up to 250 teeth. Dolphins breathe through a  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blowhole_(biology)" \o "Blowhole (biology)" blowhole on top of their head. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate_trachea" \o "Vertebrate trachea" trachea is  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anterior" \o "Anterior" anterior to the brain. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolphin_brain" \o "Dolphin brain" dolphin brain is large and highly complex, and is different in structure from that of most land mammals.Unlike most mammals, dolphins do not have hair, except for a few hairs around the tip of their  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostrum_(anatomy)" \o "Rostrum (anatomy)" rostrum (beak) which they lose shortly before or after birth. The only exception to this is the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boto" \o "Boto" Boto river dolphin, which has persistent small hairs on the rostrum Dolphins' reproductive organs are located on the underside of the body. Males have two slits, one concealing the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penis" \o "Penis" penis and one further behind for the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anus" \o "Anus" anus. The female has one genital slit, housing the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vagina" \o "Vagina" vagina and the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anus" \o "Anus" anus. Though the exact methods used to achieve this are not known, dolphins can tolerate and recover from extreme injuries, such as  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shark" \o "Shark" shark bites. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healing" \o "Healing" healing process is rapid and even very deep wounds do not cause dolphins to  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemorrhage" \o "Hemorrhage" hemorrhage to death. Furthermore, even gaping wounds restore in such a way that the animal's body shape is restored, and  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection" \o "Infection" infection of such large wounds seems rare. SensesMost dolphins have acute  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyesight" \o "Eyesight" eyesight, both in and out of the water, and they can hear frequencies ten times or more above the upper limit of adult human hearing. Though they have a small ear opening on each side of their head, it is believed hearing underwater is also, if not exclusively, done with the lower jaw, which conducts sound to the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_ear" \o "Middle ear" middle ear via a fat-filled cavity in the lower jaw bone. Hearing is also used for  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_echolocation" \o "Animal echolocation" echolocation, which all dolphins have. Dolphin teeth are believed to function as antennae to receive incoming sound and to pinpoint the exact location of an object Beyond locating an object, echolocation also provides the animal with an idea on the object's shape and size, though how exactly this works is not yet understood. The  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Dolphin" \o "Indus Dolphin" Indus Dolphin is effectively blind. This may be because not much light penetrates the waters of the  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_river" \o "Indus river" Indus river (due to suspended sediments), making eyes futile. The dolphin's sense of touch is also well-developed, with free nerve endings densely packed in the skin, especially around the snout, pectoral fins and genital area. However, dolphins lack an  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olfactory_nerve" \o "Olfactory nerve" olfactory nerve and lobes, and thus are believed to have no  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense_of_smell" \o "Sense of smell" sense of smell. They do have a sense of  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taste" \o "Taste" taste and show preferences for certain kinds of fish. Since dolphins spend most of their time below the surface, tasting the water could function like smelling, in that substances in the water can signal the presence of objects that are not in the dolphin’s mouth.Though most dolphins do not have hair, they do have  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_follicle" \o "Hair follicle" hair follicles that may perform some sensory function. The small hairs on the rostrum of the Boto river dolphin are believed to function as a  HYPERLINK "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somatosensory_system" \o "Somatosensory system" tactile sense possibly to compensate for the Boto's poor eyesight. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphins_and_Humans.html" Dolphins and Humans Humans have been interacting with dolphins for as long as we have known of their existence. In the beginning, human interaction was mainly limited to hunting dolphins. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphins_in_Captivity.html" Dolphins in Captivity Learn how dolphins in captivity behave compared with wild dolphins. Stories of boys riding dolphins date all the way back to Roman times, and even today scientific researchers sometimes ride wild dolphins they’re studying. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Learning_from_Dolphins.html" Learning from Dolphins While dolphin research is still in its infancy, the wealth of information that we may one day glean from these magnificent creatures is unimaginable. One lesson that we can learn from them today, however, could be a key to the survival of our civilization. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphins_in_the_Military.html" Dolphins in the Military Dolphins in the military. We've heard for years that Dolphins are incredibly intelligent creatures. It turns out that they are even intelligent enough to serve in the United States Navy, and have been doing so since 1960. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphins_in_Mythology.html" Dolphins in Mythology Dolphins in Mythology. Dolphins have been viewed as somehow magical for millennia by humans. They’re one of the only animals that appear to play, leaping out of the water and doing tricks, and the bottlenose dolphin even seems to grin widely at everything. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphins_Rescuing_Humans.html" Dolphins Rescuing Humans Dolphins rescuing and helping humans. Dolphins are social animals, living in groups called pods and taking loving care of their babies. They’re also known to be very friendly to humans. HYPERLINK "http://www.dolphins-world.com/Dolphin_Assisted_Therapy.html" Dolphin Assisted Therapy Dolphin Assisted Therapy. Originating in 1978 by Dr. David Nathanson, Dolphin Assisted Therapy (DAT) has been used as a therapeutic approach to increase speech and motor skills in patients who have been diagnosed with developmental, physical, and/or emotional disabilities, such as mental retardation, Down syndrome, and autism.Dolphin Facts -animalstats- |  |  |  |  | MALE | FEMALE | YOUNG | SOCIAL UIT |  | bull | cow | calf | group |  | GROUP | HOME | HABITAT | FAVOURITE FOOD |  | pod | temperate and tropical oceans | ocean | fish, squid |  | TOP SPEED | LIFESPAN | AVG. LENGTH | AVG. WEIGHT |  | 35 mph | 30 - 40 years | male 8 feet female 7.5 feet | 500 - 650 pounds |  | ESTRUS | GESTATION | BIRTHWEIGHT | BIRTH LENGTH |  | unknown | 12 months | 40 - 60 pounds | 3 - 3.5 feet |  | BORN | # OF YOUNG | AT BIRTH | BABY COLOUR |  | tail first | 1 | immediately active | paler than adult |  | WEANED | INDEPENDENT | MATURITY | ENDANGERED? |  | 12 - 16 months | 5 years | 6 - 10 years | no |  | Animal Facts Encyclopedia.com |  |  |  |  |
